People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It’s hard to talk about.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you having thoughts of killing yourself?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 26 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]

Even being in my condo—something I worked really hard for—sometimes I just sit there and feel like I’m going to cry.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I stopped going to my salsa class, and I haven’t really painted. My paints are still out on the table, but I just look at them and… nothing. Like they’re not even mine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve felt pretty low, but hurting myself… I don’t think so. It’s hard to talk about.\n\nDoctor: Are you having thoughts of killing yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not killing myself. I just feel really low sometimes, but… I don’t have a plan or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I’ve had rough times before, but I don’t know if it was like this.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen before this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess when the mortgage stuff became real. Since then it’s been about a month of… going from really wound up to just crashing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot and feeling unable to stop the worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I worry about things, yeah, but I’m not sure if it’s like… unable to stop. It just kind of comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Do your low mood and sleep problems feel mainly related to the mortgage situation?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yeah. It feels like it started when the mortgage thing became real… and for about a month now it’s been this really obvious back-and-forth between feeling all charged up and then just crashing.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still getting to work, but I’ve been making mistakes with reconciliations, enough that my supervisor talked to me about it. And I’ve kind of stopped going to salsa class or seeing friends. I just… don’t really feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after the mortgage problem began?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it pretty much started after the mortgage issue got real. The obvious up-and-down cycle has been about a month now.\n\nDoctor: When you say you feel “all charged up,” do you mean you have times when your mood is unusually high or energetic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. Earlier this month I had times where I felt kind of lit up, like I could handle anything. But I was also getting irritated really fast—snapping at my mom or even my supervisor over small questions that normally wouldn’t bother me.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt all charged up, did you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a few nights I only slept maybe three or four hours, but I still got up feeling really wired. Like I didn’t even need coffee.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt charged up, were you more talkative than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. My sister said I was talking really fast, like about the mortgage stuff—equity, taxes—and then even paint colors, and she couldn’t really get a word in. I could kind of feel myself just… pushing to keep talking.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt charged up, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I was doing more than usual. After work I’d come home and just start cleaning or making renovation lists, checking interest rates, thinking up side-job stuff. It was like I couldn’t really shut it off.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up times, did you feel unusually confident or like you could do things other people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I did. I started feeling like I could pay the condo down really fast, pass the CPA exams, maybe even run a bookkeeping thing on the side. At the time it felt like… I just couldn’t fail.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up times, were you ever hospitalized because of how you were acting or feeling?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I’ve never been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up times, did your behavior cause serious problems at work, financially, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I still go to work, but I’ve made some reconciliation mistakes… enough that my supervisor pulled me aside. And I’ve kind of been avoiding salsa class and seeing friends. I just don’t feel like myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
